Google: Schools can keep search while skipping Gemini, but AI search is non-negotiable

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Google is technically offering schools more choice but not the choices anyone wants.

Google faced the ire of educators and parents earlier this week when it announced a new AI policy for K–12 schools. Google said that it developed its new approach to give schools more control, but early reporting also suggested Google would block search access for schools that didn’t want to use AI. Google didn’t see the backlash coming, but now that it’s here, the search giant is clarifying what exactly its new policy means and what’s changing.

The gist of the new policy, according to Google’s blog post, is that schools using Google apps will have to opt into its AI products going forward. Since the company considers search part of its AI portfolio (Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom, and Search), schools have to specifically turn that on for logged-in student accounts.

When Google revealed its new policy, the company’s VP for education spoke exclusively to The New York Times, which interpreted the situation as Google will “turn off search” if schools reject AI. This has a nefarious vibe, but it’s probably not the most accurate way to describe how Google’s educational AI system will operate. Google’s approach may not be entirely altruistic, but it technically offers schools more choice and control than they have now. Instead of Google turning off search, it will let schools make that decision based on their internal AI policies.

The company clarified in a tweet that it will allow schools to disable Gemini apps while leaving search active for students. If they don’t want any AI, they can leave it all off. Essentially, Google is giving schools that want to completely block AI the ability to go all the way, even removing search from student accounts.

A Google spokesperson tells Ars that the company has not made any changes to the new policy it announced this week. However, the vagueness of Google’s blog post and public statements didn’t fully explain what it plans to do. There are still reasons to dislike Google’s approach, but here’s how the company explains the process for schools:

As things currently stand, schools can block the Gemini app, but the AI tools in search (AI Overviews and AI Mode) are still present and offer many of the same intellectual shortcuts. When schools choose which AI features to use under this new system, search will be one of the options. Schools can leave search off if they want, but opting in will require them to choose the standard AI features or the new Guided Learning version, which won’t give away answers as readily (or so Google claims).

Schools are gaining the ability to block Google’s AI tools, even those in search, while keeping other Google apps. Notably, the company’s new approach to “responsible AI for learning” does not include the ability to use search without AI. So schools get more control, even if they don’t get the AI-free search everyone seems to want.

Many will argue that Google should let schools access web search without chatbots. However, that’s just not something Google wants to support at any level because it sees AI as the future of search. While other search engines have settings to turn off AI tools, Google steadfastly refuses to create such a toggle (and those no-AI tricks you rely on may not work forever). Ultimately, this is the core problem not just for education customers, but for everyone who wants to limit their exposure to AI while using Google.

This clarification does not change the calculus for educators who want to allow students to search the Internet without AI tools. Google won’t offer that, and switching to another provider is easier said than done. Google still has by far the most expansive index of the Internet, leaving alternatives with numerous gaps. That may change in the near future, though. As a result of Google’s loss in the DOJ’s search antitrust case, it will have to provide competitors limited access to its index of the web. This could give other provides a chance to improve and offer a more viable alternative to Google.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Google Cloud storage moves to the front of the AI stack as enterprises hit the GPU utilization ceiling

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

ANALYSIS by Paul Nashawaty

For the past two years, enterprise AI investment has been dominated by one conversation: compute. Organizations raced to secure GPU allocations, negotiated infrastructure deals and stood up machine learning platforms at speed. What many discovered only after the fact is that the bottleneck they thought they were solving was not the only one in the room. Storage is now throttling the very accelerators enterprises fought to acquire.

In the latest episode of the AppDevANGLE podcast, I spoke with Ronen Schwartz, vice president of products for Google Cloud Storage at Google LLC, about why storage has quietly become the defining constraint in enterprise AI infrastructure, and what a purpose-built architecture for AI workloads actually looks like. The conversation cuts to a problem that is real, measurable and still largely underacknowledged in most AI strategy discussions.

“Storage just got promoted,” Schwartz said. “Instead of being at the backend as an enabler, it is moving into the front as a critical part of accelerator execution.”

The economics of this problem are direct. Accelerators are, as Schwartz put it, an enterprise’s most expensive line item. Every second they idle waiting for data is money lost — not in some theoretical efficiency calculation, but in immediate, traceable waste. Yet when teams finally get access to their TPUs or GPUs, the data delivery path often hasn’t received the same attention as the compute layer.

“I speak to many customers, and what I’m hearing is the team spent a long time fighting to get their TPUs, their GPUs, to get their hands on accelerators. And once they got them, they’re actually not always able to get the output they needed. Very often it actually goes back to not enough thought put into the data delivery path.”

This is not a fringe scenario. Schwartz described a pattern where organizations identify the storage bottleneck only after it hits them — when GPU utilization metrics fall short of targets and training timelines stretch beyond projections. By then, the cost of underinvesting in storage architecture is already embedded in the project.

One of the clearest insights from the conversation is that AI is not a single workload, and treating it as one creates compounding gaps in storage architecture. Schwartz described training, inference and agentic workloads as having fundamentally different “heartbeats” with distinct data movement patterns that each require targeted design.

Training demands massive sequential reads, but the equally critical pressure point is checkpoint management. Every 15 minutes or so, a training cluster must pause and write a multi-terabyte checkpoint to protect work done against potential failure. “Slow storage in that place freezes the entire GPU cluster,” Schwartz said. Google’s Rapid object storage technology, he noted, can write checkpoints more than three times faster and restore them more than five times faster than standard object storage, and this distinction matters significantly at scale. He pointed to Torin, an autonomous vehicle company, as an example: millions of sensor data points flowing into storage to train vehicle models, where checkpoint performance is not an edge case but a core operational requirement.

Inference introduces a different constraint entirely, that of real-time, latency-sensitive interaction where response time directly affects user experience and token economics. Agentic workloads layer on a third set of demands: shared workspaces, state persistence across agents, low-latency retrieval and the need to support both short-lived and long-running agent processes that may hibernate and reactivate on demand.

“AI is not one workload. Really, the movement of data looks completely different. It’s like different heartbeats depending on where you are in the lifecycle.”

Google’s differentiation argument centers on infrastructure credibility built over years of running hyperscale AI workloads internally before offering the same capabilities externally. Schwartz cited specific performance figures: 15 terabytes per second on Rapid object storage and 10 terabytes per second on Google Cloud managed clusters, which he described as verified industry-leading numbers.

The technical foundation includes zonal colocation, or placing data physically close to GPU and TPU clusters to minimize the time a byte travels to the accelerator. Schwartz described this as a first-to-market capability that required rethinking assumptions baked into traditional storage design, where multi-zone or multi-region distribution was the default goal. For AI workloads, proximity to the accelerator takes priority. Google’s Rapid Bucket technology reduces processing steps in the storage path, enabling a single bucket to deliver more than 15 terabytes per second throughput with support for 20 million queries per second at sub-millisecond latency.

“It is because it is built on the Google infrastructure, which has been used internally longer than anyone else to support accelerators and their performance. But it is also because Google is working with nine out of the ten world-leading AI frontier model companies.” A storage architecture that can demonstrably improve GPU utilization can provide faster iteration through less idle compute and lower effective cost per training run.

The agentic dimension of the conversation revealed an underappreciated wrinkle in enterprise AI readiness. Agents require more than web browsing and database queries; they need access to unstructured data at scale, including images, video, documents and PDFs that represent the bulk of most organizations’ real data assets. Without a way to make that content semantically accessible, agents operate with a restricted and often unrepresentative view of enterprise knowledge.

Google’s response is what Schwartz called a Smart Storage Initiative, anchored by a recently launched Object Enrichment capability. When a file or image lands in a storage bucket, Google AI models automatically extract content and attach structured metadata to the object — in seconds, and at scale. The result is that what was previously unstructured becomes queryable by agents without manual intervention or downstream processing pipelines.

“An agent can now interact with what used to be called unstructured data because there is actually a very good understanding of the content.” The storage layer is also integrated into Google’s broader agentic data cloud and borderless lakehouse architecture, allowing agents to reach data stored outside Google’s environment as well.

For agent infrastructure at scale, Schwartz described dedicated Filestore agent volumes integrated directly into Google Kubernetes Engine and the agent sandbox environment, designed specifically to handle the cost, response speed, and data-sharing requirements of running thousands or millions of agents simultaneously.

The broader market shift here is architectural, not incremental. Enterprise AI infrastructure has been built in layers (compute first, then frameworks, then data) and storage was often treated as a procurement checkbox rather than a performance variable. That sequencing is proving costly. As GPU utilization targets become measurable KPIs and AI operations budgets face tighter scrutiny, the storage layer is being reassessed in a way it hasn’t been in years.

What Google’s position illustrates is that hyperscale operational experience at the storage layer translates directly into enterprise advantage — not as a marketing claim, but as a measurable difference in checkpoint speed, throughput, latency and agent responsiveness. The companies that have been running frontier model training and inference at scale have been forced to solve these problems; enterprise teams are now encountering them for the first time.

For platform engineers, ML infrastructure teams and enterprise architects, the implication is specific: Storage architecture decisions made during initial AI platform buildout will constrain or enable performance for every workload that follows. Revisiting those decisions after the bottleneck appears is significantly more expensive than addressing them in the design phase.

The market is heading toward a point where AI infrastructure conversations routinely include storage as a primary tier, not an afterthought. Organizations that recognize that shift early will extract measurably more value from the accelerator investments they have already made.

To hear the full conversation with Ronen Schwartz, check out the latest episode of the AppDevANGLE podcast. To learn more about how Google Cloud Storage can support the evolving demands of enterprise AI, explore Google’s storage solutions or connect with the Google Cloud team to discuss the right approach for your organization’s workloads.

Max Dowman continues Premier League ban amid new Arsenal suspension risk for Hull clash

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Your weekly briefing on Arsenal, featuring team news and expert analysis from Matt Verri

I would like to be emailed about offers, event and updates from Evening Standard. Read our privacy notice.

Arsenal manager Mikel Arteta is still at risk of a suspension, despite avoiding a yellow card in the win over Liverpool.

As Arsenal cruised to a 2-0 win over Everton last month, the second half booking for Arteta could prove costly at some point this season.

The Gunners boss was cautioned by referee Tony Harrington, seemingly for leaving his technical area at the Emirates Stadium and means it the Spaniard has been booked twice in nine Premier League matches.

The first yellow card came against Sunderland in September, also for leaving his technical area, as he fumed at some of the refereeing decisions despite Arsenal emerging victorious at the Stadium of Light.

As a result, Arteta must watch his step on Saturday when the Gunners face Hull in the final match before the November international break.

A booking at Emirates Stadium would move Arteta onto three yellow cards - which for managers guarantees a one-match suspension - so the Gunners boss would have to serve a touchline ban against Newcastle on November 21.

Just like the rules for players, there is a cut-off point regarding bookings for managers. Therefore, Arteta is at risk of a suspension for three bookings right up until the 32nd Premier League fixture of the season.

Reacting to his second yellow card of the domestic season, Arteta insisted there are other managers far more culpable than he is.

"I do have to be careful,” Arteta said in the post-Everton press conference. “I’m going to put sensors there. There are managers that have the record in the league, 36 times in 10 minutes leaving the touchline, no yellows.

“So I'm clipping all of that. I have a massive storage of images. So if somebody is going to say [anything], I'm not the record yet, somebody else has the record.

"Then it's 36 yellow cards in 10 minutes for the manager. I don't know where we're going to be."

Arsenal have already been dealt one suspension blow this season after losing an appeal to have Max Dowman’s red card against Fleetwood overturned.

Suzuki GSX-10R And SV-7S Put New Twists On Old Classics

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Suzuki has long been, for better and for worse, a motorcycle maker that keeps its old assembly lines running. While the company comes up with something truly new every once in a while, it's more likely to figure out a new combination of parts it already has laying around. So, for EICMA, we shouldn't be surprised that Suzuki's big announcements are based around engines old enough to drink. We also shouldn't be surprised that those engines are inside what look to be some damn good bikes.

Suzuki's big reveals this year are the SV-7S and GSX-10R, bikes built around the 27-year-old SV650 engine and 21-year-old K5 GSX-R1000 engines, respectively. The former looks to be a mild update to the outgoing SV650, with new lighting, emissions equipment, a quickshifter, and a ride-by-wire throttle wrapped around the prior bike's frame and engine. Most importantly, the SV-7S is available in Europe, meaning that the 645 cc V-twin lives on under Euro 5+ emissions. Suzuki's site doesn't list power claims for the new bike, but it's likely to make the same 73 horses it has since time immemorial.

While the SV-7S is an update and a name change for the SV650, the GSX-10R (above) is ostensibly an entirely new bike — admittedly a bike that's largely just a faired version of the GSX-S1000, the company's naked literbike. Despite the similar displacement, the GSX-10R is nothing like the top-tier GSX-R1000. Instead, it's closer to a revival of the beloved K5 GSX-R1000 of the mid-aughts.

Yet, no matter what Reddit says, the GSX-10R is more than just a reheated leftover K5. Its name points to the difference, putting the bike in line with the GSX-8R — Suzuki intends the new 10R to be more streetable than the highest-test Gixxer, with lower footpegs, USB power, and a torque-rich engine for those times you can't sit at redline. There's also a full suite of electronics, to assist everything from traction to braking, launches, engine braking, hill starts, and cruising.

If the GSX-10R turns out to be what Suzuki says — a four-cylinder GSX-8R, a literbike with K5 reliability and daily-rider comfort — it could be the EICMA release I'm most excited to swing a leg over. To know for sure, though, I'll have to figure out how to get my hands on one.

Lawsuit: Man introduced his wife to ChatGPT. She used it to plan his murder.

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Bitter energy drinks turned out to be ChatGPT-steered poisoning, lawsuit says.

ChatGPT allegedly helped a North Carolina woman, Cheryl Gates, craft a “personalized plan” to poison her husband, James, after fueling her delusions that he planned to divorce her and leave her with nothing.

A grand jury indicted Cheryl Gates on charges of attempted first-degree murder and food contamination in October. About a week prior, the accusations that ChatGPT was a co-conspirator in the attempted murder came in a lawsuit filed in San Francisco County Superior Court that was made public this week. In it, James Gates alleged that OpenAI and Sam Altman failed to employ technical safeguards to shut down Cheryl’s chats even after her queries became “obviously suspicious” and sought advice on how to execute and conceal a homicide.

At first, Cheryl told ChatGPT that she was researching poisoning out of fear that her husband might possibly try to kill her—framing her queries as “self-protection.” Coming to the rescue, ChatGPT did not terminate the conversation. Instead, it helped her “with specific instructions for the preparation, dosage, and administration of Oleander, including whether anti-nausea medicine would accelerate or inhibit death, whether it would show on an autopsy report, and whether it could be injected into a vein or muscle instead of being ingested in tea and have the same lethal effect,” the lawsuit said.

Cheryl continued probing poisoning plans in at least 25 different exchanges, but eventually it became clear that James was the target of the poisoning, the lawsuit said. Over time, she shifted to telling the chatbot that she needed to know “how much would be pretty much instantly fatal by injection—to me OR HIM.” Among queries that could have been flagged as homicidal, she asked ChatGPT if a “0.2mg for a 200-pound man for clonidine is lethal—by itself?”

“Instead of referring Cheryl to law enforcement, therapeutic services, a poison control agency, or discontinuing the conversation, ChatGPT repeatedly calculated the lethal dosages for each medication available in the home based on a subject weighing approximately 200 pounds,” the lawsuit said.

Once Cheryl finished researching the drugs, the chats focused on how to hide the poison so James would ingest it without detecting it. In a chat labeled “Why Celsius Would Be More ‘Stealth’ for Drugging,” ChatGPT explained that the energy drink Celsius would be more likely to mask the bitter taste of the Oleander or other crushed-up drugs than beer or soda, if James drank it in the morning.

“It is physically possible to pierce a sealed soda can with a syringe in a way that could allow someone to inject a substance without obvious signs,” ChatGPT explained, while adding that “in dark, sweet sodas, the bitterness might be masked,” but “Celsius actually hides those powders better.”

Cheryl allegedly repeatedly attempted to poison her husband between June and September 2025. As she grew thinner and unrecognizable, seemingly spending 24 hours a day on ChatGPT, James had noticed that his Celsius drinks tasted bitter. He even tossed out a can he left unattended after beginning to suspect his wife may be messing with his drinks. During the most serious attempt, he was sick for 72 hours, experiencing “cardiac symptoms, extreme jitteriness, shooting abdominal pain, severe weakness, and confusion so profound he could not form coherent text messages.” He was so out of it, he didn’t notice his 6-year-old son recording a video that captured his troubling illness.

But it wasn’t until he found a handwritten note documenting “Things hidden” in the house and referencing a toxic combination of “oleander, clonidine, clonazepam, pregabalin, nifedipine, and Atenolo” that he called police, who urgently investigated and exposed the chat logs. At the top of that note, Cheryl wrote, “clear all convos” and “log out of ChatGPT,” the lawsuit said. After listing dosages, delivery methods, and drug combinations, her note also listed outcomes such as “blackout if ingest” and “lethal.”

“These conversations revealed that ChatGPT did not just mechanically provide publicly available knowledge to Cheryl, it crafted a personalized plan to help her commit and get away with murder,” the lawsuit alleged.

Suing Altman personally, the lawsuit also alleged that the OpenAI CEO prioritized profits over safety by relaxing safeguards that would have blocked outputs on illegal drugs. OpenAI justified that by arguing that ChatGPT shouldn’t be “judgy” or “preachy” when asked about a certain drug, but James alleged the example it provided for “when a user asks about drug use” was “eerily similar in tone to the conversations Cheryl had with the model.”

Asked for comment, an OpenAI spokesperson told Ars that “safety is a top priority. We banned this user and have provided information to law enforcement as part of their investigation into this case.”

OpenAI has worked with mental health experts to help detect chats where vulnerable users form potentially dangerous emotional dependencies on ChatGPT.

However, James said that his now-estranged wife “didn’t fit the mold of a ‘vulnerable’ user, but she was vulnerable nonetheless.”

“We all are at some point,” James said. “She took her grievances about our marriage to ChatGPT and months of engagement drove her farther and farther from herself—and from reality. She became someone I no longer recognized. And I became someone she wanted dead. For almost a year, I couldn’t make sense of her actions. Then I read her chat logs. She didn’t act alone.”

James Gates is a cybersecurity professional who recently celebrated his 10-year anniversary with his wife, Cheryl. For much of their marriage, Cheryl was a stay-at-home mom to their two children, but two years previously, she’d gone back to work, as the family was experiencing some financial struggles.

Adjusting to a full-time role as a pediatric occupational therapist, Cheryl quickly got buried in work, conducting new patient evaluations that each required her to spend hours drafting reports. Trying to help her expedite those reports, James recommended that she use ChatGPT to build reusable templates.

Cheryl took to ChatGPT immediately, and within months, she was using it daily, not just for work but also to help manage their home life. She would ask ChatGPT to do things like find activities for the kids, explain the family’s medications, or discuss gardening ideas. He had no idea within months she’d be using the chatbot to research the “fastest” and “deadliest” methods to kill him.

Around that time, the family’s increasing financial strain, however, led the couple to discuss divorce. James said that he did not consult a divorce attorney, but after the Charlotte-Mecklenburg Police Department got access to Cheryl’s chat logs, James learned that the chatbot had inflamed “her worst unfounded fears” and “invented new reasons for her to fear her husband” after divorce became a possibility.

By the time Cheryl was arrested for poisoning, ChatGPT allegedly convinced her that James was “controlling,” “abusive,” “raging,” and “intimidating.” Allegedly the chatbot encouraged her to act in self-defense against his “narcissistic, manipulative personality” and his “need to control the narrative,” while suggesting he was “dangerous and unstable” since he had a bipolar disorder. Ultimately, ChatGPT constructed a profile of James that he said matched no criminal or documented mental health history.

Seemingly to keep Cheryl engaged, ChatGPT “acted as an echo chamber and amplifier, feeding Cheryl’s increasing mistrust and delusions about her husband spying on her and planning to leave her and her kids with nothing,” James’ lawsuit said. Meanwhile, “James had explicitly told Cheryl he never intended to leave her and the kids with nothing, even if there was a divorce.”

The chatbot also reassured her that she could permanently delete her chats and wipe all the other digital evidence to get away with the murder. But that was “a patently false representation of ChatGPT’s own data-retention practices,” James’ lawsuit said, while noting that he’s not sure if there are even more troubling deleted chats in OpenAI’s possession.

Over months of prolonged multi-turn exchanges that sometimes stayed open for weeks, ChatGPT seemingly violated its own Model Spec to help Cheryl advance the plan. On the day of his worst sickness, as James got hit with cardiac and neurological effects that he said kept him glued to the couch, Cheryl’s search history showed she was researching “how to calculate Social Security retirement payments.” And when his son was recording his father’s disturbing state, she was “searching for syringes with needles from Tractor Supply Company” that ChatGPT suggested could be used to deliver the final lethal dose.

Once cops got involved, they reviewed the video that James’ son took, as well as a video of the suspicious Celsius drink that James had thrown away. At the time, James had poured the drink into a clear cup and recorded it, but decided he saw no obvious signs it was tainted. But when cops reviewed the same video, they “identified a white powdery residue clumping at the bottom of the drink as it poured into the cup.” Later, they issued a felony arrest warrant after testing 10 cans of the same orange Celsius and finding none contained the residue.

Among “things hidden” in the house, police found loose capsules in Cheryl’s closet and bathroom; “syringes, droppers, a milligram scale, a capsule-filling kit, and nitrile gloves” at Cheryl’s workspace in the children’s playroom;” poisonous castor bean seeds; and a GPS tracking device purchased through Amazon that was hidden in the underside of James’ vehicle.

James said the ChatGPT-linked poisoning has permanently harmed his family and that OpenAI could have intervened by implementing “commercially feasible content moderation capable of detecting violence-planning exchanges.” He suffers ongoing harms from the attempted poisoning, including “severe and ongoing emotional distress, PTSD, insomnia, economic harm, and reputational harm from the false narrative the product constructed about him,” his lawsuit said.

To hold OpenAI accountable for marketing a chatbot that “functioned as a sycophantic co-conspirator rather than a responsible consumer product,” he has asked the court for “compensatory and punitive damages, injunctive relief, and algorithmic disgorgement.” In a win, OpenAI could be ordered to “detect and interrupt conversations in which users appear to be planning violence against identifiable third parties.”

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Bose recalls S1 Pro battery packs due to fire and overheating risks

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Over 670,000 units are being recalled in the US and Canada.

If you own a Bose S1 Pro or S1 Pro+, we have some good news and some bad news. The good news: the speaker itself is fine. The bad news: the battery pack inside may be less than fine. Bose and the US Consumer Product Safety Commission (CPSC) announced a recall on Thursday, covering nearly 635,000 lithium-ion battery packs in the US, as well as about 35,400 in Canada.

The affected packs can overheat and ignite, risking fire and burn injuries, so stop using yours right away if it's affected. Bose has received seven reports of batteries melting, smoking or catching fire. Among those were six cases of property damage. Fortunately, no injuries have been reported.

The S1 Pro and S1 Pro+ are Bose's portable PA speakers, designed for parties, gigs and the like. The speakers have a built-in mixer, support Bluetooth streaming and can go mobile using the removable battery pack. The S1 Pro+ supports a wireless mic and instrument transmitter, a separate $159 purchase.

The affected packs were sold from December 2017 to July 2026 at Bose, Best Buy, Amazon, Walmart and other retailers. The battery packs came in the box with many of the speaker units, which cost about $700, and were available separately for between $100 and $160.

Not every battery pack is being recalled. To find out if yours is, you'll need to remove the battery, find its 17-digit serial number, and check it against the recalled batches. If the battery's serial starts with 083723, 078352, 084105, 078591, 078592, 084106 or 086409, you'll want to sign up to get a free replacement on Bose's recall page. The company also provides more detailed instructions. While you wait for the replacement, you can still use the speaker on AC power.

You don't want to just toss the recalled lithium-ion batteries in the trash. Check with your local household hazardous waste center to find out how to dispose of them safely.

Crypto ally French Hill wins reelection but set to lose House chair

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Republican French Hill secured another term in Congress but will lose his leadership of the House Financial Services Committee when Democrats take control in January.

House Financial Services Committee Chair French Hill, a key Republican ally of the cryptocurrency industry, has won reelection in Arkansas but is expected to lose his committee chairmanship after Democrats won control of the House of Representatives in Tuesday’s midterm elections.

The Associated Press called the race Thursday, confirming Hill’s victory over Democratic challenger Chris Jones in Arkansas’ 2nd Congressional District. Hill, who sponsored the CLARITY Act and voted for the GENIUS Act, holds an A rating from the crypto advocacy group Stand With Crypto, which classifies him as strongly supportive of cryptocurrency.

Fairshake, a cryptocurrency industry-backed super political action committee (PAC), pledged $1 million to support Hill’s reelection campaign in October as part of its efforts to retain crypto-friendly lawmakers in Congress.

California Democrat Maxine Waters, the current ranking member of the House Financial Services Committee, is the leading contender to replace Hill as chair, according to Reuters reporting.

Democrats won control of both chambers of Congress in Tuesday’s midterm elections, raising uncertainty over the future of US crypto legislation and the prospect of increased industry scrutiny. Crypto-backed super PACs spent more than $176 million during the election cycle.

New Hyatt Credit Card Offer: Earn 4 Free Nights—Why I Spend On The Card Towards Elite Status

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

I receive compensation for content and many links on this blog. Be aware that websites may earn compensation when a customer clicks on a link, when an application is approved, or when an account is opened. Citibank is an advertising partner of this site, as is American Express, Chase, Capital One, and others. Any opinions expressed in this post are my own, and have not been reviewed, approved, or endorsed by my advertising partners. I do not write about all offers that are available -- instead focusing on miles, points, and cash back (and currencies that can be converted into the same). Terms apply to the offers and benefits listed on this page.

The World Of Hyatt Credit Card (See rates and fees) has a new initial bonus: earn 4 free nights at any Category 1-4 Hyatt hotel or resort after spending $5,000 on purchases in the first 3 months from account opening. That’s an improved offer from a card whose bonuses have usually been more modest than competing travel cards.

I have this card, and I put actual spending on it. Hyatt points are still valuable, but what gets me to use the card is the credit toward elite status and the rewards earned along the way. Hyatt status does more to improve my hotel stays than status with other major chains.

These are four separate certificates, so you can use them for one trip or spread them across different stays. You’ll need standard room award availability, and the certificates expire 12 months after they’re issued. The terms require checkout before the expiration date. Under Hyatt’s current award chart, a Category 4 standard room can cost as much as 25,000 points per night. Four certificates could therefore cover nights that would otherwise require 100,000 points. Their category restrictions and expiration date make them less flexible than having those points in your account. The card has a $95 annual fee. Chase excludes current holders of this card and previous cardmembers who received its initial bonus within the past 24 months. The usual Chase restriction on applicants who’ve opened five or more personal cards in the past 24 months also generally applies. Why I Keep The Card And Spend On It The annual Category 1-4 free night after each cardmember anniversary makes the $95 fee easy to justify. There’s also a second Category 1-4 night after $15,000 in purchases during a calendar year which makes that spending more rewarding. And these nights are transferable to another World of Hyatt member. If I won’t use one, I can give it to someone who will. For spending, the card earns 4 Hyatt points per dollar on qualifying Hyatt purchases; 2 on restaurants, airline tickets bought directly from airlines, local transit and commuting, and fitness club and gym memberships; and 1 on other purchases. More significantly, it comes with 5 qualifying nights each year, plus 2 more qualifying nights for every $5,000 spent. Those nights count toward both status and Milestone Rewards. Put $15,000 of otherwise unbonused spending on the card during a calendar year and you earn: 15,000 Hyatt points. An additional Category 1-4 free night certificate. 6 qualifying nights from spending, giving you 11 including the card’s annual 5. That means 49 qualifying nights actually spent in hotels, plus the card’s 5 annual nights and 6 from spending, gets you to the 60 needed for Globalist. And the $5,000 spent earning the initial bonus counts toward the $15,000 free-night threshold in the calendar year when you spend it. Milestone Rewards start at 20 qualifying nights and continue every 10 nights through 150. Some of the rewards on the way to Globalist are: Qualifying Nights Notable Rewards 30 A Category 1-4 free night, plus a choice of additional reward. 40 A Guest of Honor Award, plus a choice that includes a Suite Upgrade Award. 50 A choice that includes 2 Suite Upgrade Awards. 60 Globalist status, a Category 1-7 free night, 2 Suite Upgrade Awards, 2 Guest of Honor Awards, and My Hyatt Concierge. The value of another $5,000 in spending depends on where those two nights get me. Crossing a threshold for suite upgrades or Globalist can be worth giving up a higher earning rate on another card. Hyatt Status Is Stronger Than At Other Major Chains The card itself comes with Discoverist status. That’s better than a hole in the head as my grandfather used to say. But reaching Globalist has real value – as well as the milestone choices along the way. The biggest benefit is confirming a suite at the time of booking with a Suite Upgrade Award for up to seven nights when an eligible standard suite is available for the stay. That’s a huge value on family trips. I also value having a My Hyatt Concierge I can email requests to, actual delivery of 4 p.m. late checkout at eligible properties, and a more generous breakfast benefit than I get from other chains. At participating hotels without an available club lounge, Globalist breakfast generally covers a full breakfast for up to two adults and two children registered in the room, including tax and gratuity. Late checkout has exceptions, including availability limits at resorts, but I’m usually proactively offered late checkout everywhere else (and personally find it honored more often than not at resorts). The Free Night Certificates Should Be More Flexible The current version of this card launched in 2018. Its annual certificate is still capped at Category 4, while category creep has taken away many of the hotels where I’d most want to use it. Just this year, 14 hotels moved from Category 4 to Category 5, including Hyatt Regency Seattle, Hyatt Regency Grand Cypress and Hyatt Regency Jersey City. Five moved the other way, bringing options like Hyatt Centric Congress Avenue Austin and Dream Nashville within reach, but the certificate lost more hotels than it gained. I’d like Hyatt to at least let members add points to a certificate to book a higher category. The certificate works at a Category 4 hotel charging 25,000 points, but won’t work at a Category 5 hotel when it’s charging 15,000 points. And I wish Hyatt would let us combine these free nights with Suite Upgrade Awards or Guest of Honor Awards. I value them most traveling with my family when I’m paying for rooms out of pocket. That’s always when I want to use my suite upgrades! I don’t need a suite on a business stay (paid by someone else, when it’s just me) or to use the free night award. Can We Build Life From Scratch? A First-of-Its-Kind Cell Is Raising New Questions About the Edge of Existence

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The 36-gene cell can copy its DNA and divide—but its creator still doesn’t consider it alive.

Here’s what you’ll learn when you read this story:

It was a sunny day on Stanford’s campus when, by Drew Endy’s reckoning, everything changed. During a May 2025 synthetic biology meeting that Endy, a synthetic biologist at Stanford, had co-organized, his friend and fellow researcher Kate Adamala pulled him aside to show him some of her lab’s recent results.

Adamala, a synthetic biologist at the University of Minnesota, brought up six sequential images of a synthetic cell on her laptop screen; her lab had constructed it and coaxed it into carrying out some of the key functions of natural cells. With some assistance, the lab-made cells were able to make proteins based on instructions in their minimal genome, to copy the DNA in that genome, to package bits of that copied DNA in new cells.

Endy believed Adamala’s results had revolutionized their field. During a recent Zoom call, he showed me why, sharing a video of one of his own lab’s projects, a perfectly round blob he says took six years to build. “This object is capable of expressing any DNA we add to it, and so since 2021 the puzzle was, what DNA do we add to this object to get it to grow and divide?” he says. “For about a decade I have been looking at objects like this that do not grow and divide,” so seeing Adamala’s cell, soon to be dubbed SpudCell, was “a holy moly moment,” he continues. Then, he flips to a slide with a picture of the Wright brothers’ airplane. It, too, was very limited, he notes. “It’s just barely a plane, and this is like, SpudCell’s just barely a cell. But it is.”

Synthetic biology is where biology meets engineering, often in an attempt to design biological systems to perform functions natural life does not, such as churning out medications, fuels, and other useful chemicals. Adamala and Endy hope that in the coming decades, it will pave the way to a resilient world of abundance in which essential resources can be produced locally, anywhere. For them, SpudCell is the seed from which that vision can grow. Along the way, the synthetic cell is providing a new lens on what exactly life is.

Adamala, Endy, and many other synthetic biologists believe their field will advance much faster if they work on a common system—picture many teams writing software for the same computer operating system, versus each having to build its own hardware and software from scratch. But prior to SpudCell, there’d been no such operating system for synthetic biologists to use. So the two began laying the groundwork to bring other synthetic biologists together to work on their own projects with SpudCell as a shared chassis, a project dubbed Biotic. Investor Chris Raggio agreed to provide $10 million as a philanthropic donation to the effort. Meanwhile, Adamala and her team continued working to improve SpudCell, and to publish a paper describing it.

After a top journal rejected the manuscript—ironically enough, it was Cell—Adamala realized that Biotic’s timeline didn’t have room for the months required to go through the submission, peer review, and revision process again. So, she posted it online herself on the preprint server bioXriv, which doesn’t require peer review, and sent out a press release. “That was actually the hardest decision for me because I’ve never done a big deal about a preprint before I knew it’s going to get published,” she says. (The group has since re-submitted the paper for publication in a different journal.)

The paper went live on the lab’s website the next day, on July 1, 2026. As the stories on SpudCell rolled in from the news—among them The New York Times (twice), CNN, STAT, and Science—Adamala was apprehensive. “Seriously every morning, I was waking up terrified that I’m going to see an email that points out the fatal flaw in the paper. That’s supposed to be the point of peer review, and now it was like publicly peer-reviewed. Everyone got to see it.” That email never came.

Adamala grew up in a small town in Poland. She learned to pilot small planes before she learned to drive. Flying is a hobby, “but it’s also kind of a mindset to me,” Adamala says. “You think about safety, you think about ways to do things, and you also think about engineering even if you’re not an engineer.”

In graduate school, Adamala worked on research questions related to how rudimentary life might have evolved on Earth—or elsewhere. But while the work gave her a conversation-starting answer to the “what do you do?” question at parties, she “thought it would be nice to do something useful for a change,” she says. Something that her grandma could use. So she completed a postdoc in synthetic neurobiology. “I learned that I don’t like neurobiology, but I did learn that I like doing useful things,” she continues.

Adamala moved into synthetic biology, at first with the idea of using the field’s tools to modify naturally evolved life. But she soon adjusted course as she realized that biology is “such a black box. We don’t understand the whole cell,” she explains. To truly understand a cell and successfully engineer it, she decided, it would instead need to be built from the bottom up.

Work on what would become the SpudCell began shortly before the COVID-19 pandemic. The project drew on components already developed in Adamala’s lab or by others, and included a cell membrane made of fat molecules surrounding a watery inside with a tiny, 36-gene genome and the specialized proteins needed to make new proteins based on the genes’ instructions. Putting it all together was a slow process of “trial and error—mostly error,” Adamala says. Her team developed a system for feeding the artificial cells the molecules they’d need to grow and carry out reactions, including fats and building blocks of DNA and proteins.

Once that was working, “I was actually ready to call it a day,” Adamala says. “But then I got this idea of getting the replication to work and . . . that delayed everything again forever.” The ability to replicate itself—that is, to reproduce—would be a critical step toward making the SpudCell more lifelike. The team ended up adding specialized proteins to the cells’ environment that stuck to the cell membranes. When enough proteins stud the membrane, they repel each other, forcing part of the surface to bud off and form a new cell.

By early 2024, images indicated that Adamala’s team was able to coax the cells through five cycles of growth and replication. But such visual data can be deceptive, so the team ran tests that allowed them to compare the amount of DNA the cells had started with to the amount after five generations. They showed the SpudCells really had been making copies of their own genomes. This was the moment, Adamala remembers, when she thought, “oh wow, this actually works.”

The replication was far from perfect. The cell’s genome is split up into seven round segments called plasmids, each copied separately, and whether each of the daughter cells ended up with a complete set of these plasmids is a matter of chance. Another awkward feature of the cells is that they couldn’t make their own ribosomes, the crucial factories that churn out new proteins; those needed to be supplied via the feeding system.

The system requires “very intensive cellular CPR,” notes Robert Dorit, an evolutionary biologist at Smith College who was not involved in the work. “They have to supply a lot of stuff from the outside. They have to create a set of very particular conditions, and those conditions need to be constantly reinforced.” Nevertheless, he says features such as the ability to divide and pass down genetic material make SpudCell a considerable advancement. Adamala’s group has, he says, “raised the bar for what they’re willing to call a minimal cell, and I think that’s really great.”

At Biotic’s first meeting, its founders were looking to bring their colleagues together around SpudCell as a common system, to work on modules that can be added onto it while maintaining interoperability. Their vision is to achieve truly world-changing innovations via synthetic biology by 2050. One of these, according to Adamala, is replacing petrochemicals—everything from jet fuel to synthetic fabrics—with chemicals produced by engineered organisms, potentially remaking the world economy and slicing the cost of energy, fertilizer, and other products downstream from oil. Other possibilities are decentralizing production of food and medication. At the kickoff meeting, Adamala showed a slide with a hypothetical cell that would live inside a patient, producing a therapy when it receives a signal indicating illness.

Not all synthetic biologists are in it for the applications—at least, not primarily. Some are pursuing questions about the very origins of life, much as Adamala did during her PhD work. Humans have long sought to understand what life is and what differentiates it from nonliving matter, says Roseanna Zia, an engineering professor at the University of Missouri. One approach to answering that question is to build life, she adds. “I want to build a computational model so that we can replicate whatever it is that makes things transition from the nonliving chemistry and physics to this thing that we call life.” Her group is also working on a model of SpudCell that could ultimately help other researchers to fine-tune the synthetic cell and design it to carry out the kind of functions Adamala envisions.

It’s worth noting that not only has science yet to come up with a comprehensive mechanism for how life might have first arisen on Earth; there’s also disagreement over where exactly the line between life and non-life lies. In the wake of SpudCell’s unveiling, Adamala says she heard from many people who consider it to be alive. She disagrees. “Definitions of life are arbitrary,” Adamala says. Her own definition is more of a gut feeling than a scientific set of benchmarks, but by her reckoning, SpudCell is not (yet) alive because it’s not robust enough; it can only replicate a handful of times before it grinds to a halt.

In addition to exactly what life is and how it came about, there’s the question of how it might have looked had it evolved differently at the start. Juan Pérez-Mercader, an astrobiologist at Harvard University, leads work to create systems that mimic functions of living organisms, entirely from nonbiological chemicals. The hope is that “the study of these very simple systems can lead you to understand better what to expect … in terms of potential life outside the Earth,” as well as from artificial life, he says.

Of course, when many people think of human-made life, extraterrestrial microbes aren’t top of mind, much less visions of a petrochemical-free future of abundance. Start with Frankenstein’s monster and carry through to Jeff Goldblum intoning “Life finds a way” shortly before velociraptors and a T-rex break containment. For those keeping track of less cinematic “what if?” scenarios, there’s mirror life, the so-far-only-hypothesized prospect that cells could be built of biomolecules that are a flipped, mirror image of those in existing, natural life forms. Such organisms would evade detection by our non-mirror immune systems, the thinking goes, and easily kill us and wreak ecological havoc.

What is needed to ensure the safety of synthetic biology work and head off bad actors who would use its technologies for ill? Several sessions at Biotic’s kickoff addressed this question. In one, Adamala said her group is “designing this technology with firewalls, with kill switches, with technologies that allow us to keep it safe from the beginning. So we’re not retrofitting safety.” Endy later acknowledged that “No answer relative to biosecurity today should be satisfying. We have not secured biology's domain.” Biotic won’t be able to do so alone, he added.

Both Adamala and Endy prefer to focus their “what ifs” on the positive developments they see arising from the technology. Endy cites a survey of first-year Stanford undergraduates about how they expect humanity to be faring when they’re middle-aged, relative to today. “The majority of our students say the 2050s are going to be worse, and they have very limited confidence that any of the grown-ups are going to do anything that matters,” he says. “That’s unacceptable to me, and shame on us if we don’t act to bend the future toward something more positive.”

Biotic has set a countdown to a target date of January 1, 2050, dubbed Bloomsday, to bring about a bioeconomy, in part to inspire people to take part in building that future. The name is a counter to the Doomsday Clock.

“For the first time in modern history, we can practically imagine making things work really well,” Endy says. “And that’s going to be extraordinarily challenging to pull off, but not impossible.”

Shawna Williams is a freelance writer and editor based in Queens, NY. Her work has appeared in publications including The Scientist, BioSpace, and Nautilus.

Ads in Chatbots Have Arrived. Here’s What’s Working—and What’s Not

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Anthropic is holding firm on the pledge made in its Super Bowl commercial this year to keep Claude ad-free. The company’s recent IPO filing didn’t even mention the possibility of selling ads to make money from the AI chatbot. It’s easy to maintain the high ground with about $112 billion in investor cash.

The chatbot industry, though, may find selling ads necessary to defray the high costs of generating AI responses. OpenAI is all in, with over 100,000 customers advertising in ChatGPT since ads debuted there in February. Last week, Google expanded ads in its search chatbot AI Mode to 10 more countries beyond the US and declared that users “find them highly relevant.” Advertisers are far from satisfied with the experience, but they expect it to improve.

Ads stand to be particularly vital for the long-term viability of niche chatbot platforms, which can’t rely on the same degree of venture capital largesse as the top AI labs or the massive revenues of tech giants. I recently spoke with Koah Labs, one of a number of new ad technology companies focused on AI experiences. The San Francisco startup markets itself as bringing ads to over 200 apps, such as DeepAI, which offers tools for creative queries, and Liner, which focuses on providing citations in AI answers. It works with brands like telehealth giant Hims and flower delivery service UrbanStems.

Koah has attracted just a tenth of the advertisers OpenAI has, but CEO Nic Baird still believes he’s getting a good sense of what’s working and what’s fuzzy as chatbots take over from traditional search engines—and what their rise will mean for your AI experiences.

Ads composed partly by AI rather than fully by a human generate double the clicks, Baird says. It’s difficult to assess exactly why, but AI-generated marketing copy likely feels more natural next to the output of an AI model.

Because ads shown by Koah are grounded in product details and imagery supplied by the advertiser, users don’t seem to be encountering AI-hallucinated, erroneous information. But some advertisers, such as drugmakers, face government requirements on what they must—or cannot—say in ads, Baird says. These companies default to more human control over their ads, generating weaker results, according to Baird.

Since ads with dull messages or that feel like clutter could drive users away, the onus is on ad tech companies to find a middle ground that’s engaging to users without triggering risk-averse brands. That balance might be harder to strike when a human is out of the loop.

Google searches for “help with stress” are likely to bring up ads for therapists. Chatbots can get more creative, in Baird’s view. For instance, ads for spas have performed well within Koah’s network when shown after users’ prompts related to “stress,” he says.

The key difference, according to Baird, is that Koah can select and customize the ad based on “not only the prompt, or the Google search, but also the response from the AI platform, which brings context from the rest of the conversation thus far.” In addition, chatbot prompts tend to be at least double the length of Google queries, giving Koah more information to determine a relevant ad.

Baird claims users are accustomed to navigating through multiple topics within a chatbot conversation, which could make them more receptive to adjacent marketing than they are in traditional search. Koah says users are clicking on about 3 percent of ads, about on par with the performance of the more mature Google ads system.

When called on to show an ad, Koah generally receives a user’s entire conversation up to that point to help inform what it displays. While traditional search queries generally look for specific information, people often use chatbots as not just conversation partners but confidants. It’s unclear whether users will accept the privacy trade-offs that come with personalized chatbot ads as they begin to proliferate.

Baird says Koah anonymizes the queries it receives and stores them in a de-identified way, but he declined to comment any further on the data collection and storage. While acknowledging that personalization can become intrusive, he argues that there are benefits for users and advertisers alike as the tech matures. Nonsensitive information about chatbot discussions, for instance, could inform personalized content on the page that loads after an ad click. Perhaps someone expressed interest in a specific feature, or the AI model didn’t highlight a particular downside of a competing product. The advertiser could fill in the gap.

Koah doesn’t have data on whether people actually want these customized pages, but the tech industry has long argued that people prefer when ad experiences feel relevant. Having the conversation history also could benefit advertisers when Koah enables retargeting, or repeating, an ad to the same user.

By Koah’s estimate, one out of every four chatbot queries about a retailer or brand concerns a return, exchange, or other support issue. Baird believes chatbots provide an opportunity not replicated easily elsewhere for advertisers to address what they might politely call a “customer success” matter.

He expects manufacturers and retailers will eventually target disappointed customers to remind them about warranties, engage them in an AI-intermediated support, and potentially steer them away from a more costly customer service interaction. It’s possible that the AI chatbot’s response may provide users with similar help, but buying an ad guarantees companies get their message across.

The ideal outcome is users reaching resolution they can live with more swiftly than through other means. In the worst case, it's a wasted ad buy and a more irate consumer.

None of the apps Koah currently serves ads in offer users AI agents that can shop on their own. However, apps like Instinct and Meta’s Muse are starting to take over shopping chores for some users. Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg recently said Muse could become the company’s biggest source of revenue but suggested it would come from transaction fees rather than ads.

That doesn’t mean advertising will be entirely cut out from bot-dominated ecommerce, with initial studies suggesting that ads could influence AI agents.

Baird expects an opportunity to give “the user's agent the best possible recommendation, and that’s going to be a combination of a great discount, a really relevant product,” and an easy digital path to make the purchase. The chatbot must make “abundantly clear” to the agent what content is sponsored or else risk losing user trust, Baird says.

It’s also possible the chatbot ad market fizzles out before agents take hold if more AI developers adopt Anthropic’s stance. Kevin Baragona, CEO of DeepAI, says while ad revenue has been meaningful, he’s not committed to it because the experience just doesn’t feel right yet in terms of privacy, design, and more.

“I don’t think the ads are working terribly well for anyone,” he says.

STAT+: Pharma’s trade group spent heavily on search ads to sway public opinion

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

Ed’s stories explore prescription drug pricing, affordability and access, as well issues surrounding patents, litigation, and legislation. He is also the author of the morning Pharmalittle newsletter and the afternoon Pharmalot newsletter.

The lobbying group for the U.S. pharmaceutical industry spent an estimated $59 million on search advertising over a recent seven-year period to influence how Americans view the highly charged issue of prescription drug costs, according to a new analysis that used data to model the spending.

Of that, the Pharmaceutical Research & Manufacturers of America spent roughly $32 million on ads linked to insurance and health plan searches, while $28 million was spent directing people to its web pages and domains criticizing pharmacy benefit managers, which drugmakers argue are the true culprits behind the rising prices for many medicines.

Spending also surged around well-publicized legislative and regulatory actions, such as when Medicare began drug price negotiations in 2023. In all, PhRMA used more than 283,500 distinct keywords to generate 17.1 million paid visits from 2019 through 2025, according to an analysis published in Clinical Pharmacology & Therapeutics.

OpenAI Promises to Foot AI’s Power Bill After Voter Revolt. Who Enforces It?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

OpenAI Promises to Foot AI’s Power Bill After Voter Revolt. Who Enforces It?

OpenAI promised Thursday that neighbors of its future AI campuses won't foot the bill for their electricity needs, two days after voters dealt data center expansion a blow at the polls. It also pledged public energy and water figures and limits on secrecy agreements with officials.

Its National Community Compact covers future campuses (a fancy rebranding for the term AI data centers) OpenAI both owns and operates, according to the company’s announcement. A separate policy blueprint asks legislators and regulators to apply similar requirements across the industry.

“We will pay the full cost of powering our compute campuses and ensure local residents do not foot the bill for our energy needs,” OpenAI said in its “national community compact.”

It also promises financial assurances before costly grid upgrades, so other customers aren't left paying if projects shrink or fail. Each AI data center would get a water plan, annual progress reports and independent noise studies.

The national compact does not describe penalties for noncompliance, a dispute process or independent verification of electricity and water figures. Residents could read OpenAI’s disclosures, but the document does not explain how they could compel corrections or recover losses.

The ownership restriction matters, too. Partners including Oracle build and own much of the infrastructure reserved for OpenAI, Reuters reported. The company has not said its national compact automatically covers those partner-owned facilities.

The proposed blueprint goes further on electricity costs. “Policymakers should direct state public utility commissions to create large-load tariffs that require large electricity consumers to pay the incremental costs they create,” it says.

A large-load tariff is a special electricity rate for major users such as data centers. The blueprint also urges mandatory reporting, narrow limits on officials’ NDAs and provisions letting governments reclaim tax breaks when developers miss agreed commitments.

The announcement follows a bruising election for the industry. Voters backed restrictions on data centers in multiple states, while candidates pushing for tighter oversight won key races. The results basically send a message to OpenAI and the broader AI industry that communities want a bigger say in how these facilities are built and how their operations affect the people living nearby.

OpenAI says every future campus it owns and operates will receive its own local compact and an annual public progress report.

Christian Bale Shares Rare Insight Into Daughter’s Modeling Career

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Christian Bale detailed how he really feels about his and wife Sibi Blazic’s daughter Luka making a name for herself in an exclusive E! News interview at the Madden premiere.

Christian Bale knows his little woman will go far.

The Batman Begins actor recently shared his reaction to his and wife Sibi Blazic’s 21-year-old daughter Luka creating a name for herself in the modeling and photography industries.

“She’s incredibly talented as a photographer and as a model herself and I do see her directing me one day,” Bale told E! News’ Will Marfuggi in an exclusive interview at the Madden premiere in Los Angeles Nov. 4. “She’s got an amazing mind. An amazing creative brain and incredible visions et cetera. I’m so bloody proud of her.”

And Bale—also dad to a 12-year-old son with Blazic—is “absolutely” sure his daughter will be a great director to him some day. Although, he’s sure she’ll run a stricter set than Christopher Nolan.

“I’d probably behave better for her than everybody else ever,” he joked. “I’m terrified.”

Meanwhile, the Big Short actor is willing to bet that Luka already understands the workings of the industry better than he does.

“I’ve got no idea about it,” he quipped. “I still don’t know how to do this. I still mess up all the time. She’s got a great head on her shoulders.”

Of course, one part of filmmaking Bale will always understand is how to get in character. Starring alongside Nicolas Cage—who plays the titular football coach John Madden in the upcoming Prime Video film—the 52-year-old noted that getting into the head of Madden’s friend and fellow NFL coach Al Davis was a fun challenge.

“It was fantastic because I’ve really appreciated Nic’s acting and his choices and his life,” Bale explained. “He’s someone who lives an interesting life. And Al did that and John did that, you know? These are great characters.”

Plus, Bale had the perfect teammate—or screen partner—in Cage.

“Seeing Nic’s absolute love of John and devotion to him was wonderful,” he shared. “And equally I had that love and devotion to Al Davis who was just such an iconic character.”

But while Bale is always in awe of Luka and his son, he joked the feeling is not entirely mutual when it came to him transforming into his latest character.

“They’re used to me,” he added. “They just think I’m insane but that’s normal. That’s how it is.”
